Inference of Online Newton Methods with Nesterov’s Accelerated Sketching
Haoxuan Wang, Xinchen Du, Sen Na

Abstract
Reliable decision-making with streaming data requires principled uncertainty quantification of online methods. While first-order methods enable efficient iterate updates, their inference procedures still require updating proper (covariance) matrices, incurring O ⁡ ( d 2 ) O(d^{2}) time and memory complexity, and are sensitive to ill-conditioning and noise heterogeneity of the problem. This costly inference task offers an opportunity for more robust second-order methods, which are, however, bottlenecked by solving Newton systems with O ⁡ ( d 3 ) O(d^{3}) complexity. In this paper, we address this gap by studying an online Newton method with Hessian averaging, where the Newton direction at each step is approximately computed using a sketch-and-project solver with Nesterov’s acceleration , matching O ⁡ ( d 2 ) O(d^{2}) complexity of first-order methods. For the proposed method, we quantify its uncertainty arising from both random data and randomized computation. Under standard smoothness and moment conditions, we establish global almost-sure convergence, prove asymptotic normality of the last iterate with a limiting covariance characterized by a Lyapunov equation, and develop a fully online covariance estimator with non-asymptotic convergence guarantees. We also connect the resulting uncertainty quantification to that of exact and sketched Newton methods without Nesterov’s acceleration. Extensive experiments on regression models demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method for online inference.

* Equal contribution.

1
Introduction

We consider the stochastic optimization problem:

\min_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{P}}[F(\bm{x};\xi)],

(1.1)

where
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a stochastic objective and
F(\cdot;\xi):\mathbb{R}^{d}\\
\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is its realization with a random sample
\xi\hskip-1.0pt\sim\hskip-1.0pt\mathcal{P}
. Problem (
1.1
) is simple but serves as a fundamental building block for many decision-making tasks in statistics and machine learning, such as online recommendation
(
35
)
, precision medicine
(
29
)
, energy control
(
56
)
, and portfolio allocation
(
18
;
10
)
. In these applications, (
1.1
) is often interpreted as a parameter estimation problem, where
\bm{x}
denotes the model parameter,
\xi
corresponds to a data sample, and the population minimizer
\bm{x}^{\star}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\bm{x})
represents the underlying true model parameter.

The ubiquity of streaming data in modern applications has made online methods particularly attractive, where only one
single
sample is used per step. One of the most fundamental online methods is the stochastic gradient descent (SGD)
(
50
;
28
)
:

\bm{x}_{t+1}=\bm{x}_{t}-\varphi_{t}\nabla F(\bm{x}_{t};\xi_{t}).

(1.2)

Numerous studies have explored both non-asymptotic and asymptotic convergence properties (e.g.,
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{2}]\leq\varphi_{t}
and
\bm{x}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\rightarrow}}\bm{x}^{\star}
) of SGD and many accelerated variates
(
49
;
31
;
44
;
2
;
39
)
. These results show the consistency of SGD as an estimate of
\bm{x}^{\star}
. However, point estimation alone is insufficient for reliable decision-making; one must also quantify the uncertainty of the estimation procedure, e.g., via standard errors and confidence intervals.
This need has inspired a recent series of studies on online uncertainty quantification and inference of SGD. In particular,
51
;
47
proposed averaging SGD iterates,
\bar{\bm{x}}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\bm{x}_{i}/t
,
and established the asymptotic normality of
\bar{\bm{x}}_{t}
. This result has since been extended to broader gradient-based methods
(
55
;
37
;
16
;
11
)
.
Given the asymptotic normality, online inference largely reduces to estimating the associated limiting covariance matrix, for example via plug-in or batch-means procedures
(
9
;
58
;
60
;
52
;
26
)
. There also exist alternative inference procedures that bypass explicit covariance estimation but are also less statistically efficient, such as bootstrapping
(
19
;
38
;
57
;
32
)
and random scaling
(
36
;
33
;
15
)
.

While first-order methods (
1.2
) admit efficient iterate updates, their inference procedures still require updating proper (covariance) matrices, incurring
O(d^{2})
time and memory complexity. Moreover, SGD is delicate to ill-conditioning and noise heterogeneity of the problem. It has been largely observed that inference of SGD can suffer from clear undercoverage even for problems of small dimensions as
d=20

(
9
;
58
;
30
)
. This costly and significantly challenging online inference task offers a nice opportunity to apply higher-order (quasi-)Newton methods, which exploit the objective curvature information via the update
(
4
;
6
)
:

\bm{x}_{t+1}=\bm{x}_{t}+\varphi_{t}\Delta\bm{x}_{t},\;\text{with}\;B_{t}\Delta\bm{x}_{t}=-\nabla F(\bm{x}_{t};\xi_{t})

(1.3)

where
B_{t}\approx\nabla^{2}f(\bm{x}_{t})
is an estimate or approximation of the objective Hessian.
There has been a growing interest in quantifying the uncertainty of (
1.3
).

34
treated
B_{t}^{-1}
as a preconditioning matrix and established the asymptotic normality of the last iterate
\bm{x}_{t}
under the convergence of
B_{t}
. They further showed that
\bm{x}_{t}
attains
optimal statistical efficiency
when
B_{t}\rightarrow\nabla^{2}f(\bm{x}^{\star})
, corresponding to online Newton methods.
7
;
5
;
8
;
22
specialized (
1.3
) to regression problems and derived the asymptotic normality for the averaged Newton iterate
\bar{\bm{x}}_{t}
, and
42
;
20
extended (
1.3
) to constrained problems.

Suppose the Hessian estimate is accessible (otherwise, it can be approximated by first- or zeroth-order information via quasi-Newton or finite-difference schemes
(
12
;
53
)
). The major concern of (
1.3
) lies in solving the Newton system, which is prohibitively expensive with an
O(d^{3})
time complexity.
As such,
41
introduced an online sketched Newton method that approximately solves the Newton system via a
sketch-and-project
solver, thereby reducing the time complexity to
O(d^{2})
. The authors quantified the uncertainty of sketched Newton method by establishing the last-iterate asymptotic normality. To bridge the gap between uncertainty quantification and practical inference,
30
further proposed an online, consistent limiting covariance matrix estimator that is constructed solely from sketched Newton iterates, without inverting any matrices.
Together, these two works are the first attempts to make Newton methods practically viable for online inference tasks, achieving robust outperformance over SGD.

On the other hand, recent advances in randomized linear solvers have shown that the sketch-and-project solver used in aforementioned works, originating from
54
;
24
;
45
;
46
;
14
, can be further sped up via
Nesterov’s acceleration
, without increasing the order of per-iteration cost
(
23
;
13
)
.
Particularly,
13
showed that the expected squared solution approximation error decays exponentially at the rate
1-\mu_{t}
for unaccelerated methods, while at
1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}
for accelerated methods. Here,
\mu_{t},\nu_{t}
are problem parameters satisfying
1\leq\nu_{t}\leq 1/\mu_{t}
(that is,
\mu_{t}\leq\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}\leq\sqrt{\mu_{t}}
). See (
2.7
) for details.
Consequently, for the extreme case of
\nu_{t}=1
, accelerated sketching improves the rate of the sketch-and-project solver from
1-\mu_{t}
to
1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}}
, mirroring the effect of Nesterov’s acceleration in SGD. This improved computational efficiency motivates a natural but largely unexplored question:

How does accelerated sketching affect statistical efficiency of inference in online sketched Newton methods?

In this work, we answer this question by establishing global almost-sure convergence and local asymptotic normality of online Newton methods with Nesterov’s accelerated sketching. In contrast to the above sketching literature that focuses on computational efficiency of sketching solver, we focus on how accelerated sketching affects uncertainty quantification and inference of online Newton methods.
Specifically, we quantify the uncertainty of (
1.3
) from both random data and accelerated sketching. Under standard moment conditions on the estimation noise, we first show that

1/\sqrt{\varphi_{t}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star})\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\Sigma^{\star}),

(1.4)

where the limiting covariance
\Sigma^{\star}
is characterized by the solution to a Lyapunov equation that depends explicitly on the sketching distribution (cf. (
4.2
)). This characterization discloses a
computational-statistical trade-off
induced by accelerated sketching solver, with two special scenarios:

(a)

When accelerated sketching is completely discarded, our results reduce to those of exact Newton method
(
34
)
, and
\Sigma^{\star}
recovers the covariance of averaged SGD estimator
(
47
)
, which is known to be
statistically minimax optimal

(
11
)
.

(b)

When accelerated sketching does not yield a provable accelerated rate (i.e.,
\mu_{t}\nu_{t}=1
),
\Sigma^{\star}
recovers the covariance of the unaccelerated sketched Newton method
(
30
)
. Notably, no provable acceleration is much more general than specific unaccelerated sketching scheme used in that work; in particular, momentum may still be employed but does not offer an improved convergence rate.

To carry out practical inference based on normality (
1.4
), we further develop a fully online estimator of the covariance
\Sigma^{\star}
and establish its consistency. The asymptotic normality together with consistent covariance estimation fully resolve the inference task for online sketched Newton methods with Nesterov’s acceleration.
We demonstrate theoretical findings on broad regression problems, illustrating the practicality and advantages of the proposed inference procedure.

Technical challenges.

Our inference of online Newton with Nesterov’s accelerated sketching overcomes several technical challenges building on existing works.

First
, covariance estimation relies on the non-asymptotic convergence rate for the fourth moment of the Newton iterate error
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{4}]
(cf. Lemma
4.5
), which is a higher-order guarantee than commonly studied second-moment bound
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{2}]

(
34
;
5
;
8
;
22
)
.

Second
, our method requires quantifying uncertainty from both data sampling and randomized linear solver, unlike existing inference methods in which randomness comes solely from data sampling
(
9
;
58
;
11
;
26
)
. The sketching solver is executed for a fixed number of steps, so its algorithmic randomness does not vanish asymptotically and thus inevitably affects the limiting distribution (see (
4.2
)).

Third
, prior work
30
;
41
studied unaccelerated sketching, while we extend their analysis to Nesterov’s accelerated sketching. The extension is highly technically involved:

(a)

Nesterov’s acceleration introduces an additional sequence of momentum co-states; thus, the solver evolves as a
2d
-dimensional state-co-state recursion, rather than an independent, projection-based state update as in standard sketch-and-project methods
(
14
, (1.1))
.

(b)

Accelerated sketching involves a random, time-varying, asymmetric
2\times 2
block matrix acting as a linear operator

(cf. (
3.2
)), while without acceleration, it reduces to a symmetric projection matrix in the
(1,1)
block.
Accordingly, many key properties no longer hold under acceleration. For example, while contraction of the
(1,1)
block can be established in unaccelerated case due to favorable boundedness of projection matrices, this property fails for the full asymmetric operator. As such, we leverage the Cayley–Hamilton theorem and tools from the theory of similar matrices and Kronecker products to derive a recursion for the spectral radius and analyze the contraction of the
(1,1)+(1,2)
block, corresponding to marginal state evolution (Lemmas
3.6
,
3.7
).

(c)

Acceleration introduces conditionally deterministic, but random parameters
(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t},\gamma_{t})
governing the stepsize and momentum, induced by the linear operator in
(b)
. In contrast, these parameters are deterministic in unaccelerated settings. To show asymptotic normality, we have to explore their
non-asymptotic
convergence rates (e.g.,
|\alpha_{t}-\alpha^{\star}|=O_{p}(\sqrt{\varphi_{t}})
), ensuring that the randomness of these parameters leads to only higher-order terms relative to the randomness from data sampling and sketching (Lemma
4.2
).
In other words, when
t
is large, the sketching solver at iteration
t
can be viewed as operating at
\bm{x}^{\star}
with optimal parameters.

To our knowledge, all above technical challenges and our resolutions do not appear in existing works on either sketched Newton methods or online inference.

Notation.

We let
\|\cdot\|
denote the
\ell_{2}
norm for vectors and spectral norm for matrices.
For two sequences
\{a_{t},b_{t}\}
,
a_{t}=O(b_{t})
implies that
|a_{t}|\leq c|b_{t}|
for sufficient large
t
and some positive constant
c
;
a_{t}=o(b_{t})
implies that
|a_{t}/b_{t}|\to 0
as
t\to\infty
. We also denote
O_{p},o_{p}
the order in probability.
We let
I
denote the identity matrix,
\bm{0}
denote the zero vector or matrix, and
\bm{1}
denote the all-one vector; their dimensions are clear from the context. For a series of compatible matrices
\{A_{i}\}
,
\prod_{k=i}^{j}A_{k}=A_{j}A_{j-1}\cdots A_{i}
if
j\geq i
and
I
if
j<i
. For two symmetric matrices
A
and
B
,
A\succeq B
means
A-B
is positive semidefinite; and
\lambda_{\min}(A)
(
\lambda_{\max}(A)
) denotes the smallest (largest) eigenvalue
A
. We also denote
f_{t}=f(\bm{x}_{t})
and
f^{\star}=f(\bm{x}^{\star})
(similar for
\nabla f_{t},\nabla^{2}f_{t}
, etc.).

2
Online Newton with Accelerated Sketching

We introduce online Newton method with Nesterov’s accelerated sketching. The method simply adopts the update (
1.3
) with
B_{t}
defined as the averaged Hessian estimate. Instead of solving the Newton system of (
1.3
) exactly, we employ an accelerated sketching solver to reduce time complexity.

2.1
Online Newton with Hessian averaging

At each outer iteration
t
, we draw a sample
\xi_{t}\sim\mathcal{P}
and compute the gradient and Hessian estimates
g_{t}=\nabla F(\bm{x}_{t};\xi_{t})
and
H_{t}=\nabla^{2}F(\bm{x}_{t};\xi_{t})
. As standard in studies of Newton methods, we suppose the Hessian estimate
H_{t}
is accessible; otherwise, it can be approximated by quasi-Newton or finite-difference schemes, and our analysis extends to those settings with proper adjustments
(
34
;
42
)
.
We define
B_{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}H_{i}
as the Hessian average based on samples
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=0}^{t-1}
, admitting an online update:

B_{t}=\left(1-1/t\right)B_{t-1}+H_{t-1}/t.

(2.1)

Inspired by the techniques of online variance reduction, Hessian averaging has been widely used in stochastic Newton methods to accelerate convergence rates
(
1
;
40
;
34
;
5
;
41
)
.

With
B_{t}
in (
2.1
), we then aim to solve the Newton system:

B_{t}\Delta\bm{x}_{t}=-g_{t}.

(2.2)

In general, solving (
2.2
) costs
O(d^{3})
complexity, making the method impractical.
We compute
\Delta\bm{x}_{t}
approximately in
O(d^{2})
via Nesterov’s accelerated sketching.

2.2
Sketch-and-Project with Nesterov’s acceleration

We now focus on (
2.2
). To ease notation, we drop the outer iteration index
t
in this subsection and introduce the accelerated sketching solver
\texttt{NASketch}(B,-g;\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\tau)
, as summarized in Algorithm
1
. Here,
(B,-g)
denote the left-hand side matrix and right-hand side vector of linear system, respectively;
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
are solver parameters related to the momentum stepsize; and
\tau
is the number of sketching steps. The solver will output an approximation of
\Delta\bm{x}
. We refer a detailed introduction of the solver to
23
;
13
.

Algorithm 1

Sketch-and-Project with Nesterov’s Acceleration:
\texttt{NASketch}(B,-g;\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\tau)

1:

Target:

B\Delta\bm{x}=-g
;

2:

Initialize:
set initial values
\bm{z}_{0}=\bm{v}_{0}=\bm{0}
;

3:

for

j=0,1,\ldots\tau-1

do

4:

\bm{y}_{j}=\alpha\bm{v}_{j}+(1-\alpha)\bm{z}_{j}
;

5:

Generate a sketching vector/matrix
S_{j}\sim S\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times s}
;

6:

\bm{\omega}_{j}=BS_{j}(S_{j}^{\top}B^{2}S_{j})^{\dagger}S_{j}^{\top}(B\bm{y}_{j}+g)
;

7:

\bm{z}_{j+1}=\bm{y}_{j}-\bm{\omega}_{j}
;

8:

\bm{v}_{j+1}=\beta\bm{v}_{j}+(1-\beta)\bm{y}_{j}-\gamma\bm{\omega}_{j}
;

9:

end

for

10:

Output:

\bm{z}_{\tau}
.

For each (inner) sketching iteration
j
(at fixed outer iteration
t
), we draw a sketching vector/matrix
S_{j}\sim S\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times s}
with
s\ll d
. Without acceleration, the vanilla sketch-and-project method admits the update:

\bm{z}_{j+1}=\bm{z}_{j}-BS_{j}(S_{j}^{\top}B^{2}S_{j})^{\dagger}S_{j}^{\top}(B\bm{z}_{j}+g),

(2.3)

where
\dagger
denotes the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse;
\bm{z}_{j}
is the current state; and
BS_{j}(S_{j}^{\top}B^{2}S_{j})^{\dagger}S_{j}^{\top}(B\bm{z}_{j}+g)
is the sketching direction, based on the observation that the above update is equivalent to solving a sketch-and-project problem:

\bm{z}_{j+1}=\arg\min\|\bm{z}-\bm{z}_{j}\|^{2}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad S_{j}^{\top}B\bm{z}=-S_{j}^{\top}g.

With acceleration, the method involves an additional momentum co-state
\bm{v}_{j}
. Given the state-co-state pair
(\bm{z}_{j},\bm{v}_{j})
, we first compute the sketching direction at their middle point
\bm{y}_{j}=\alpha\bm{v}_{j}+(1-\alpha)\bm{z}_{j}
as

\bm{w}_{j}=BS_{j}(S_{j}^{\top}B^{2}S_{j})^{\dagger}S_{j}^{\top}(B\bm{y}_{j}+g).

Then, the pair
(\bm{z}_{j},\bm{v}_{j})
is updated as

\begin{cases}\bm{z}_{j+1}=\bm{y}_{j}-\bm{\omega}_{j},\\
\bm{v}_{j+1}=\beta\bm{v}_{j}+(1-\beta)\bm{y}_{j}-\gamma\bm{\omega}_{j}.\end{cases}

Clearly, by setting
\alpha=0.5
,
\beta=0
,
\gamma=1
, accelerated sketching reduces to unaccelerated sketching (
2.3
).

Parameter setup and accelerated rate.

23
;
13
proposed controlling
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
by two problem parameters
(\mu,\nu)
as

\alpha=1/(1+\gamma\nu),\quad\beta=1-\sqrt{\mu/\nu},\quad\gamma=1/\sqrt{\mu\nu},

(2.4)

where
(\mu,\nu)
are defined as (or lower, upper bounds of)

\mu=\inf_{\bm{a}\neq{\bm{0}}}\frac{\bm{a}^{\top}Z\bm{a}}{\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{a}},\quad\quad\nu=\sup_{\bm{a}\neq{\bm{0}}}\frac{\bm{a}^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}Z^{-1}\tilde{Z}]\bm{a}}{\bm{a}^{\top}Z\bm{a}},

(2.5)

with
\tilde{Z}\coloneqq BS(S^{\top}B^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{\top}B
and
Z\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
being a projection matrix and its expectation, taken over the randomness of sketching only. (Recall that we drop the outer iteration index
t
; thus, the expectation is conditional on
\bm{x}_{t}
and sample
\xi_{t}
).

23
shows:

1\leq\nu\leq 1/\mu=\|Z^{-1}\|,

(2.6)

implying that
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\beta\in[0,1)
,
\gamma\geq 1
.

Furthermore,
13
showed that accelerated sketching enjoys an exponential rate:

\mathbb{E}\left\|\bm{z}_{j}-\bm{z}\right\|^{2}\leq 2(1-\sqrt{\mu/\nu})^{j}\left\|\bm{z}_{0}-\bm{z}\right\|^{2},

(2.7)

where
\bm{z}=\Delta\bm{x}=-B^{-1}g
is the exact solution and the expectation is again taken over randomness of sketching only. The rate
1-\sqrt{\mu/\nu}
in (
2.7
) improves the rate
1-\mu
of unaccelerated sketching
(
24
)
. We note that the provable acceleration degrades if and only if
\gamma=1
, i.e.,
\mu\nu=1
, which is substantially more general than the setting of unaccelerated sketching scheme (
2.3
) (momentum may be employed but does not offer an improved rate).

With the above sketching solver, online Newton performs:

\hskip-1.42271pt\bm{x}_{t+1}=\bm{x}_{t}+\varphi_{t}\cdot\texttt{NASketch}(B_{t},-g_{t};\alpha_{t},\beta_{t},\gamma_{t},\tau),

(2.8)

where
\varphi_{t}=C_{\varphi}/{(t+1)^{\varphi}}
is a vanishing stepsize sequence.

3
Assumptions and Global Convergence

In this section, we introduce assumptions and prove global almost-sure convergence of online Newton with Nesterov’s accelerated sketching. Unlike aforementioned sketching literature that focuses on the solver-level convergence rate in (
2.7
), we study the convergence of outer Newton iterates
\bm{x}_{t}
, and more importantly, their uncertainty quantification and inference in Section
4
.
This focus shift requires significantly different guarantees and analytical techniques even for Algorithm
1
:
13
uses Gaussian universality to derive a sharp bound on
\nu
, while we abstract Nesterov’s acceleration via a random, asymmetric linear operator that is closely tied to the limiting covariance matrix
\Sigma^{\star}
in (
1.4
). To analyze that operator, we employ the Cayley-Hamilton theorem along with tools from similar matrices and Kronecker products to derive a recursion for its spectral radius.

3.1
Assumptions

We define the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t-0.5}=\sigma\left(\{\xi_{i},\{S_{i,j}\}_{j}\}_{i=0}^{t-1}\cup\xi_{t}\right)
that contains all the randomness of
\bm{x}_{0:t}
and
\xi_{t}
; and
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma\left(\{\xi_{i},\{S_{i,j}\}_{j}\}_{i=0}^{t}\right)
containing the randomness of
\bm{x}_{0:(t+1)}
.
The first assumption regards the strong convexity and Lipschitz smoothness of the objective.

Assumption 3.1
.

We assume that
f
and realizations
F(\cdot;\xi)
are twice continuously differentiable such that for any
\bm{x}
,
\gamma_{H}\leq\lambda_{\min}\left(\nabla^{2}F(\bm{x};\xi)\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla^{2}F(\bm{x};\xi)\right)\leq\Upsilon_{H}
for some constants
\Upsilon_{H}>\gamma_{H}>0
. We also assume
\nabla^{2}f
is
\Upsilon_{L}
-Lipschitz continuous, i.e.,
\|\nabla^{2}f(\bm{x})-\nabla^{2}f(\bm{x}^{\prime})\|\leq\Upsilon_{L}\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|
for any
\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}
.

Assumption
3.1
is standard in existing inference literature
(
9
;
58
;
30
)
. The next assumption imposes a growth condition on the moment of gradient noise, which is also standard in those works.

Assumption 3.2
.

For any
t\geq 0
, we assume the gradient estimate is unbiased
\mathbb{E}[g_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=\nabla f_{t}
and satisfies a growth condition on its
q_{g}
-th moment: for some
C_{g,1},C_{g,2}>0
,

\mathbb{E}[\|g_{t}-\nabla f_{t}\|^{q_{g}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq C_{g,1}\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{q_{g}}+C_{g,2}.

We will specify
q_{g}
in theorem statements. In particular,
q_{g}=2
suffices for global convergence,
q_{g}>2
for asymptotic normality, and
q_{g}=4
for online inference; all moment conditions are standard in aforementioned literature. Similarly, we present moment conditions on the Hessian noise.

Assumption 3.3
.

For any
t\geq 0
, we assume the Hessian estimate is unbiased
\mathbb{E}[H_{t}\hskip-1.0pt\mid\hskip-1.0pt\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\hskip-1.0pt=\hskip-1.0pt\nabla^{2}f_{t}
and satisfies a growth condition on its
q_{H}
-th moment: for some
C_{H,1},C_{H,2}>0
,

\mathbb{E}[\|H_{t}-\nabla^{2}f_{t}\|^{q_{H}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq C_{H,1}\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{q_{H}}+C_{H,2}.

Assumptions
3.2
and
3.3
have been verified in
9
for various regression problems.
We finally impose an assumption on the sketching distribution.

Assumption 3.4
.

For any
t\geq 0
, we assume
\{S_{t,j}\}_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}S
satisfying
Z_{t}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[B_{t}S(S^{\top}B_{t}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{\top}B_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\succeq\gamma_{S}I
for some
\gamma_{S}>0
, and
\{S_{t,j}\}_{j=0}^{\tau-1}
are independent of data sample
\xi_{t}
.
Further, we assume the moment of the condition number of
S
is finite:
\mathbb{E}[(\|S\|\|S^{\dagger}\|)^{q_{S}}]\leq\Upsilon_{S}
for
\Upsilon_{S}>0
.

As analyzed in
41
;
30
,
q_{S}=1
suffices for asymptotic normality and
q_{S}=2
for inference.
Note that the above expectations are taken over the randomness of the sketching matrix
S
. The lower bound on expected projection matrix
Z_{t}
is commonly required to ensure convergence of sketching solvers, both with and without acceleration
(
24
;
23
;
13
)
. All conditions in Assumption
3.4
easily hold for various sketching distributions, including Gaussian sketching
S\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\Sigma)
and Kaczmarz sketching
S\sim\text{Unif}(\{\bm{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d})

(
54
)
.

3.2
Guarantees of accelerated sketching

We state some results of Algorithm
1
. Note from Section
2.2
that, for each
t
, we draw
\tau
sketching matrices
\{S_{t,j}\}_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\sim\\
S
. Define
\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}=B_{t}S_{t,j}(S_{t,j}^{\top}B_{t}^{2}S_{t,j})^{\dagger}S_{t,j}^{\top}B_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and denote their shared conditional expectation, taken over the randomness of sketching, by
Z_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
. We also define parameters
(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t},\gamma_{t},\mu_{t},\nu_{t})
as in (
2.4
)-(
2.5
). Now, let us construct an asymmetric
2\times 2
block matrix

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{t,j}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1-\alpha_{t}&\alpha_{t}\\
(1-\alpha_{t})(1-\beta_{t})&\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t}\end{pmatrix}\otimes I_{d}

\displaystyle\quad-\begin{pmatrix}1-\alpha_{t}&\alpha_{t}\\
(1-\alpha_{t})\gamma_{t}&\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t}\end{pmatrix}\otimes\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d},

(3.1)

where
\otimes
is the Kronecker product. We will show in Lemma
3.5
that
\widetilde{C}_{t,j}
captures the transition of state-co-state sequence
\{(\bm{z}_{t,j},\bm{v}_{t,j})\}_{j}
of Algorithm
1
: for
t\geq 0
,
0\leq j\leq\tau-1
,

(\bm{z}_{t,j+1}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t},\bm{v}_{t,j+1}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})=\widetilde{C}_{t,j}(\bm{z}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t},\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}).

Naturally, the product
\tilde{C}_{t}\coloneqq\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\tilde{C}_{t,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d}
denotes the transition operator mapping
(\bm{z}_{t,0},\bm{v}_{t,0})
to
(\bm{z}_{t,\tau},\bm{v}_{t,\tau})
. We use
[\widetilde{C}_{t}]_{k,l}
to denote its
(k,l)
-th block, and let

\widetilde{K}_{t}\coloneqq[\tilde{C}_{t}]_{1,1}+[\tilde{C}_{t}]_{1,2}=\begin{pmatrix}I&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\tilde{C}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}I\\
I\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

(3.2)

be the matrix governing the evolution of marginal state
\bm{z}_{t,j}
. To analyze the expected error of Algorithm
1
, we further define
C_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{C}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
and
K_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{K}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
.

The first lemma makes the above statement rigorous.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any
t\geq 0
, the construction of
\widetilde{K}_{t}
in (
3.2
) admits the representation

\bm{z}_{t,\tau}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}=\widetilde{K}_{t}(\bm{z}_{t,0}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})\quad\text{with}\quad\Delta\bm{x}_{t}=-B_{t}^{-1}g_{t}.

If
\mathbb{E}[g_{t}\hskip-2.0pt\mid\hskip-2.0pt\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\hskip-2.0pt=\hskip-2.0pt\nabla f_{t}
, the conditional expectation of
\bm{z}_{t,\tau}
is

\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=-(I-K_{t})B_{t}^{-1}\nabla f_{t}.

Thus, the residual
\bm{z}_{t,\tau}-(I-K_{t})\Delta\bm{x}_{t}
form a martingale difference sequence with respect to filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Finally, if
\gamma_{t}=1
,
\widetilde{K}_{t}
has a simple form of
\widetilde{K}_{t}=\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}(I-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})
.

To control the
\tau
-step contraction of accelerated sketching solver, we analyze its associated asymmetric linear operator
C_{t}
and marginal operator
K_{t}
. We observe that the accelerated sketching dynamics defined by
C_{t},K_{t}
decouple along eigendirections of
Z_{t}
and reduce to a family of matrices:

G_{t}(z)\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1-\alpha_{t}&\alpha_{t}\\
(1-\alpha_{t})(1-\beta_{t})&\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t}\end{pmatrix}\\
-\begin{pmatrix}1-\alpha_{t}&\alpha_{t}\\
(1-\alpha_{t})\gamma_{t}&\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t}\end{pmatrix}z.

(3.3)

The following lemma makes the corresponding
spectral radius recursion
explicit via Cayley–Hamilton theorem.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
p_{t,\tau}(z)=\bm{e}_{1}^{\top}G_{t}(z)^{\tau}\mathbf{1}
for a fixed
t\geq 0
and
z\in[\mu_{t},1]
. Then,
p_{t,\tau}(z)
satisfies the second-order recursion: for all
\tau\geq 2
,

p_{t,\tau}(z)=\text{Tr}_{t}(z)\,p_{t,\tau-1}(z)-D_{t}(z)\,p_{t,\tau-2}(z),

(3.4)

with initialization
p_{t,0}(z)=1
and
p_{t,1}(z)=1-z
, where
\text{Tr}_{t}(z)=\mathrm{trace}(G_{t}(z))
and
D_{t}(z)=\det(G_{t}(z))
. Moreover, under Assumption
3.4
, the spectral radius of
G_{t}(z)
, denoted as
\rho\big(G_{t}(z)\big)
, is uniformly contractive:

\hskip-5.69046pt\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}\rho\big(G_{t}(z)\big)\leq 1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}\leq 1-\gamma_{S}\eqqcolon\rho_{\star}<1.

(3.5)

Combining the recursion (
3.4
) with the contractive spectral radius bound (
3.5
) yields

\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}\big|p_{t,\tau}(z)\big|\leq 2\tau\ (1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}})^{\tau-2}.

(3.6)

The quantity
p_{t,\tau}(z)=\bm{e}_{1}^{\top}G_{t}(z)^{\tau}\mathbf{1}
measures the magnitude of
K_{t}
along an eigendirection of
Z_{t}
with eigenvalue
z
; thus, bounding
\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}|p_{t,\tau}(z)|
leads to a direct control of
\|K_{t}\|
, where the range of
z
is ensured by Assumption
3.4
.
The recursion (
3.4
) and contraction (
3.5
) provide a tractable way of deriving the bound of
p_{t,\tau}(z)
even if
G_{t}(z)
is asymmetric; and the explicit bound in (
3.6
) quantifies how
\tau
exponentially controls
\|K_{t}\|
, as stated in the next lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

Under Assumption
3.4
, for any
t\geq 0
, we have

\|K_{t}\|\leq 2\tau(1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}})^{\tau-2}.

When
\gamma_{t}=1
, Lemma
3.5
shows that the marginal operator
\widetilde{K}_{t}
reduces to a product of
\tau
projection matrices. Lemma
3.7
further shows that the exponential rate
1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}
reduces to
1-\mu_{t}
, which coincides with the rate for unaccelerated sketching
(
24
)
. The constant factor
\tau
in (
3.6
) and Lemma
3.7
is negligible; the overall behavior of
K_{t}
is fully dominated by the exponential decay of
\tau
.

3.3
Global almost-sure convergence

With the results of accelerated sketching, we now establish global almost-sure convergence of the outer, online Newton iterates with Nesterov’s accelerated sketching.

Theorem 3.8
.

Consider the Newton scheme (
2.8
) with the stepsize
\varphi_{t}=C_{\varphi}/(t+1)^{\varphi}
satisfying
C_{\varphi}>0
,
\varphi\in(0.5,1]
. Suppose Assumptions
3.1
–
3.4
hold with
q_{g}=2
,
q_{H}=q_{S}=0
, and
\tau
satisfies
\tau(1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}})^{\tau-2}\leq\gamma_{H}/(4\Upsilon_{H})
for all
t\geq 0
. Then, we have
\bm{x}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\rightarrow}}\bm{x}^{\star}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

We note that global convergence only requires gradient estimates to have bounded variance (
q_{g}=2
), which is standard in the literature
(
21
)
. No moment conditions are needed for Hessian estimates or the condition number of the sketching distribution (
q_{H}=q_{S}=0
). The condition on
\tau
can be indeed satisfied uniformly by invoking (
3.5
); specifically,
\tau\rho_{\star}^{\tau-2}\leq\gamma_{H}/(4\Upsilon_{H})
. This condition ensures that, at each step, the approximate Newton direction from accelerated sketching solver is not too far from the exact Newton direction, at least in an average sense.

4
Uncertainty Quantification and Inference

We quantify uncertainties of online Newton with Nesterov’s accelerated sketching from both random sampling and randomized computation. We first establish asymptotic normality with a limiting covariance given by a Lyapunov equation depending on the solver operator. We then propose an online, consistent covariance estimator for practical inference goals.

4.1
Asymptotic rate and normality

We begin by showing the almost-sure convergence of the Hessian average
B_{t}
, the acceleration parameters
(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t},\gamma_{t})
, and, more importantly, the marginal sketching operator
K_{t}
. Let
B^{\star}\coloneqq\nabla^{2}f(\bm{x}^{\star})
and define
(\alpha^{\star},\beta^{\star},\gamma^{\star})
as in (
2.4
) with
(\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star})
in (
2.5
) evaluated at
B^{\star}
. Then, we let
K^{\star}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{K}^{\star}]
, where
\widetilde{K}^{\star}
is defined analogously to
\widetilde{K}_{t}
in (
3.2
) while evaluated at
(\alpha^{\star},\beta^{\star},\gamma^{\star})
and
B^{\star}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
3.8
but strengthening
q_{S}=0
to
q_{S}=1
, then we have

B_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\rightarrow}}B^{\star}
,
(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t},\gamma_{t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\rightarrow}}(\alpha^{\star},\beta^{\star},\gamma^{\star})
, and
K_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\rightarrow}}K^{\star}
.

We require finite expected condition number of
S
since the definition of
(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t},\gamma_{t})
relies on
(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})
, involving the expected condition number.
The almost-sure convergence of
K_{t}
to
K^{\star}
, together with Lemma
3.7
, ensures that our limiting covariance established in (
4.2
) is well defined, provided the number of sketching steps
\tau
is chosen properly. The following lemma further provides the convergence rates.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose Assumptions
3.1
–
3.4
hold with
q_{g}>2
,
q_{H}=2
,
q_{S}=1
;
\tau
satisfies
\tau(1\hskip-1.0pt-\hskip-1.0pt\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}})^{\tau-2}\hskip-2.0pt\leq\hskip-2.0pt\gamma_{H}/(4\Upsilon_{H})
for all
t\geq 0
; and the stepsize parameters satisfy
\varphi\in(0.5,1]
and
C_{\varphi}>0.5/\lambda_{\min}(I-K^{\star})
if
\varphi=1
. Then, we have

\max\big\{\|B_{t}-B^{\star}\|,\;|\alpha_{t}-\alpha^{\star}|,\;|\beta_{t}-\beta^{\star}|,\\
|\gamma_{t}-\gamma^{\star}|,\;\|K_{t}-K^{\star}\|\big\}=O_{p}(\sqrt{\varphi_{t}}).

To establish convergence rates in Lemma
4.2
, we have to leverage techniques for analyzing nonsmooth SGD (
11
, (10.1)) to introduce a stopping time to localize the analysis. See Appendix
B.3
for the formal definition.

Before presenting the normality result, we introduce some additional notation. Let us define two sandwich matrices

\displaystyle\Omega^{\star}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(B^{\star})^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\nabla F(\bm{x}^{\star};\xi)\nabla^{\top}F(\bm{x}^{\star};\xi)](B^{\star})^{-1},

(4.1)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\star}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[(I-\widetilde{K}^{\star})\Omega^{\star}(I-\widetilde{K}^{\star})^{\top}].

In fact,
\Omega^{\star}
is the limiting covariance of averaged SGD estimator
(
51
;
47
)
, which is asymptotically minimax optimal
(
16
;
11
)
. However, due to randomized computation,
\Gamma^{\star}
plays a crucial role in
our
limiting covariance. It depends on the accelerated sketching operator
\widetilde{K}^{\star}
(cf. (
3.2
)), and its expectation is taken over
\tau
sketching matrices
\{S_{j}\}_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\sim S
.

Now, with the notation in place, we present the following asymptotic normality of the last Newton iterate
\bm{x}_{t}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
4.2
, we have

1/\sqrt{\varphi_{t}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star})\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\Sigma^{\star}),

where
\Sigma^{\star}
satisfies the following Lyapunov equation:

\left[(I-K^{\star})-\zeta I\right]\Sigma^{\star}+\Sigma^{\star}\left[(I-K^{\star})-\zeta I\right]=\Gamma^{\star},

(4.2)

and
\zeta\coloneqq\mathbf{1}_{\{\varphi=1\}}/2C_{\varphi}
.

By standard properties of the Lyapunov equation (
27
), we know (
4.2
) always admits a unique positive semidefinite solution. This follows from the facts that
\Gamma^{\star}\succeq{\bm{0}}
and
(I-K^{\star})-\zeta I\succ{\bm{0}}
; the latter is guaranteed by the conditions on
\tau
and
C_{\varphi}
in Lemma
4.2
.

To end this subsection, we draw connections between our limiting covariance
\Sigma^{\star}
and those of exact and unaccelerated sketched Newton methods.

Proposition 4.4
.

Consider
\Sigma^{\star}
in (
4.2
) in two degenerate regimes.

(a)

When the Newton system is exactly solved (
K^{\star}=\widetilde{K}^{\star}={\bm{0}}
), we have
\Sigma^{\star}=0.5\Omega^{\star}
for
\varphi\in(0.5,1)
and
\Sigma^{\star}=\frac{C_{\varphi}\Omega^{\star}}{2C_{\varphi}-1}
for
\varphi=1
. In the latter case, setting
\varphi=C_{\varphi}=1
leads to

\sqrt{t}\cdot(\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star})\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\Omega^{\star}).

(b)

When the accelerated rate is not provably achieved (
\gamma^{\star}=1
),
\Sigma^{\star}
is reduced to the solution to

\left[(I-\mathcal{C}^{\star})-\zeta I\right]\Sigma^{\star}+\Sigma^{\star}\left[(I-\mathcal{C}^{\star})-\zeta I\right]=\mathcal{G}^{\star},

(4.3)

where
\mathcal{C}^{\star}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\star}]
and
\mathcal{G}^{\star}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[(I-\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\star})\Omega^{\star}(I-\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\star})^{\top}]
with

\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\star}\coloneqq\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}(I-B^{\star}S_{j}(S_{j}^{\top}(B^{\star})^{2}S_{j})^{\dagger}S_{j}^{\top}B^{\star})
.

Case
(a)
matches the limiting covariance matrix of conditioned SGD method
(
34
)
, indicating that the Newton estimator attains optimal statistical efficiency on par with the averaged SGD estimator. Case
(b)
matches the limiting covariance matrix of unaccelerated sketched Newton method
(
41
)
; however, the latter corresponds only to Algorithm
1
under the parameter setup
(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t},\gamma_{t})=(0.5,0,1)
. In contrast, our condition
\gamma^{\star}=1
is significantly weaker, achieved by our novel analysis.

4.2
Online covariance estimation

Building on the results of Section
4.1
, we construct in this section an online estimator for the limiting covariance
\Sigma^{\star}
.
With this covariance estimator, we can then perform online statistical inference, e.g., constructing asymptotically valid confidence intervals for the model parameters.

Inspired by prior work on covariance estimation
(
9
;
58
;
26
;
30
)
, we consider the following weighted sample covariance esti-

mator:

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\frac{1}{\varphi_{i-1}}(\bm{x}_{i}-\bar{\bm{x}}_{t})(\bm{x}_{i}-\bar{\bm{x}}_{t})^{\top}

(4.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\frac{\bm{x}_{i}\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}}{\varphi_{i-1}}-\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\frac{\bm{x}_{i}\bar{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\top}+\bar{\bm{x}}_{t}\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}}{\varphi_{i-1}}+\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\frac{\bar{\bm{x}}_{t}\bar{\bm{x}}_{t}^{\top}}{\varphi_{i-1}}.

(4.5)

From the second line above, we see that all summations admit efficient online updates similar to (
2.1
); thus,
\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}
can be constructed efficiently in an online fashion, using only accelerated Newton iterates themselves and without involving any matrix-vector products.

To show the consistency of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}
, we first need to provide non-asymptotic rates for the fourth moments of the iterate and Hessian average errors,
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{4}]
and
\mathbb{E}[\|B_{t}-B^{\star}\|^{4}]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose Assumptions
3.1
-
3.4
hold with
q_{g}=q_{H}=4
,
q_{S}=2
;
\tau
satisfies
\tau(1\hskip-0.5pt-\hskip-0.5pt\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}})^{\tau-2}\hskip-1.0pt\leq\hskip-1.0pt\gamma_{H}\hskip-1.0pt/\hskip-1.0pt(4\Upsilon_{H})
for all
t\geq 0
; and the stepsize parameters satisfy
C_{\varphi}>0
and
\varphi\in(0.5,1)
. Then, we have

\max\big\{\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{4}],\;\mathbb{E}[\|B_{t}-B^{\star}\|^{4}]\big\}=O(\varphi_{t}^{2}).

With the above lemma, we show the convergence rate of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}
in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.6
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
4.5
, the covariance estimator
\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}
defined in (
4.4
) satisfies

\mathbb{E}[\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}-\Sigma^{\star}\|]=O(1/\sqrt{t\varphi_{t}}).

Since
t\varphi_{t}\rightarrow\infty
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, Theorem
4.6
implies that
\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}
is a consistent estimator of
\Sigma^{\star}
. An immediate consequence of this consistency is the construction of confidence intervals:

P\big(\bm{w}^{\top}\bm{x}^{\star}\in\big[\bm{w}^{\top}\bm{x}_{t}\pm z_{1-q/2}\{\varphi_{t}\bm{w}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}\bm{w}\}^{0.5}\big]\big)\rightarrow 1-q

as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Here,
q_{1-q/2}
is the
(1-q/2)
-quantile of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Figure 1
:
A subset of QQ plots for linear regression with Kaczmarz (a-d) and Gaussian (e-h) accelerated v.s. unaccelerated sketching.

We empirically evaluate online Newton with Nesterov’s accelerated sketching and compare it with exact and sketched Newton
without
acceleration. We also evaluate our inference procedure based on the covariance estimator
\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}
. Inference performance is assessed by the mean absolute error, the empirical coverage rate of the constructed confidence intervals, and their average lengths over 200 independent runs. Due to space limits, we present a subset of results while complete experimental setups and results are in Appendix
C
.

Following prior literature
(
9
;
59
;
41
;
30
)
, we consider the same linear and logistic regressions with varying dimensions
d
and covariance
\Sigma_{a}
for random covariates. We also vary the sketching steps
\tau
(
\tau=\infty
corresponds to exact Newton) and sketching distributions (Kaczmarz and Gaussian).

\bullet

Covariance of accelerated vs. unaccelerated sketching.

Since accelerated sketching enhances computational efficiency of Newton methods, a natural question is
whether accelerated sketching introduces additional uncertainty to the estimation procedure
. We use QQ plots to compare the covariance matrices in (
4.2
) and (
4.3
). We examine several combinations of
(d,\tau)
, as reported in Figure
1
. From Figure
1
, we observe that the accelerated sketched online Newton produces quantiles that closely align with those of unaccelerated scheme, indicating similar empirical distributions and comparable asymptotic covariance structures. The close alignment of the two limiting covariances suggests that accelerating the sketching solver does not degrade statistical efficiency or lead to additional estimation uncertainty, an aspect largely missing in the literature.
These QQ plots also provide empirical evidence for the normality of accelerated Newton iterates (Theorem
4.3
).

\bullet

Asymptotic validity of inference.
Table
1
shows that the empirical coverage rates of the confidence intervals constructed by accelerated sketched online Newton remain close to the nominal
95\%
level across all settings. This provides numerical support for our asymptotic normality and covariance estimation theory (Theorems
4.3
and
4.6
).

\bullet

Mean absolute error.

Compared to exact Newton method (
\tau=\infty
), our inexact Newton method achieves comparable mean absolute errors in both linear and logistic regressions. This aligns with the fact that both exhibit
\sqrt{\varphi_{t}}
-consistent, implying the same leading-order rate for the estimation error. Notably, in several linear regression settings (especially when
\Sigma_{a}
is Toeplitz or Equi-correlation), our inexact Newton method attains even smaller iterate errors than exact method, with reductions varying
8.8\%
–
37.5\%
as seen in Table
1
.

\bullet

Statistical efficiency of accelerated vs. exact Newton.
As shown in Table
1
, in most settings, inexact Newton method yields slightly longer average confidence intervals than exact Newton method. This modest inflation is indeed expected, as the additional randomness introduced by the sketching solver can increase the variability of the resulting iterates and, consequently, the estimated asymptotic variance.

Overall, our inexact Newton method achieves near-nominal coverage, comparable (and in some cases improved) mean absolute errors, and only modest increases in averaged confidence interval length relative to the exact Newton method. These results suggest that our inference procedure preserves asymptotic validity, numerical accuracy, and statistical efficiency, while benefiting from faster theoretical convergence (compared to unaccelerated sketching) and lower computational costs (compared to the exact Newton). Furthermore, while
30
employs at least 10 sketching steps to achieve competitive performance in large dimensions, our method attains comparable or even superior results with at most 5 sketching steps. This reduction of sketching steps leads to substantial computational savings in practice, highlighting the practical advantages of Nesterov’s acceleration for online Newton-based inference.

6
Conclusion

We studied uncertainty quantification and inference of online Newton methods with Nesterov’s accelerated sketching. The proposed method has
O(d^{2})
time and memory complexity, matching that of first-order methods. We established global almost-sure convergence, derived asymptotic normality, and characterized the limiting covariance through a Lyapunov equation that explicitly captures the effect of the sketching solver. We further proposed a fully online covariance estimator with non-asymptotic guarantees, enabling practical inference without inverting any matrices. Our study clarifies the
computational-statistical trade-offs
induced by accelerated sketching and connects resulting inference behavior to that of exact and unaccelerated sketched Newton methods.

Appendix: Inference of Online Newton Methods with Nesterov’s Accelerated Sketching

Appendix A
Proofs of Section
3

A.1
Proof of Lemma
3.5

First, we derive the recursion of
(\bm{z}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t},\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})
for any fixed outer loop iteration
t
. In Section
2.2
, we temporarily suppress the outer iteration index and write
\{\bm{z}_{j},\bm{v}_{j},\bm{y}_{j}\}
. Below, we write
\{\bm{z}_{t,j},\bm{v}_{t,j},\bm{y}_{t,j}\}
to make the dependence on the outer index
t
explicit. By Algorithm
1
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{z}_{t,j+1}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bm{y}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{\omega}_{t,j}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{t}(\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})+(1-\alpha_{t})(\bm{z}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}(\bm{y}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})

\displaystyle=(I-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})[\alpha_{t}(\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})+(1-\alpha_{t})(\bm{z}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})],

and

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{t,j+1}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\beta_{t}(\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})+(1-\beta_{t})(\bm{y}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})-\gamma_{t}\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}(\bm{y}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})

\displaystyle=\beta_{t}(\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})+(I-\beta_{t}I-\gamma_{t}\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})(\bm{y}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})

\displaystyle=\beta_{t}(\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})+(I-\beta_{t}I-\gamma_{t}\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})(\alpha_{t}(\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})+(1-\alpha_{t})(\bm{z}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}))

\displaystyle=[(\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t})I-\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t}\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}](\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})+[(1-\alpha_{t})(1-\beta_{t})I-(1-\alpha_{t})\gamma_{t}\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}](\bm{z}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}).

Combining the above two displays, we have the following recursion formula:

\begin{pmatrix}\bm{z}_{t,j+1}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\\
\bm{v}_{t,j+1}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}(1-\alpha_{t})(I-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})&\alpha_{t}(I-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})\\
(1-\alpha_{t})(1-\beta_{t})I-(1-\alpha_{t})\gamma_{t}\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}&(\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t})I-\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t}\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}\end{pmatrix}}_{\widetilde{C}_{t,j}}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{z}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\\
\bm{v}_{t,j}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.1)

With the above formula (
A.1
), we have the following closed form of
(\bm{z}_{t,\tau}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t},\bm{v}_{t,\tau}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t})
:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\bm{z}_{t,\tau}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\\
\bm{v}_{t,\tau}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:7}}}}{{=}}\;\widetilde{C}_{t,\tau-1}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{x}_{t,\tau-1}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\\
\bm{v}_{t,\tau-1}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\end{pmatrix}=\cdots=\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\widetilde{C}_{t,j}\right)\begin{pmatrix}\bm{z}_{t,0}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\\
\bm{v}_{t,0}-\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\;=\;-\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\widetilde{C}_{t,j}\right)\begin{pmatrix}\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\\
\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\end{pmatrix}=-\widetilde{C}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\\
\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\end{pmatrix},

where the second last equality holds since we set
\bm{z}_{t,0}=\bm{v}_{t,0}=\bm{0}
in Algorithm
1
. Thus, for any fixed
\tau>0
, we have the following closed form for the sketched solver operator:

\begin{pmatrix}\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\\
\bm{v}_{t,\tau}\end{pmatrix}=(I-\widetilde{C}_{t})\begin{pmatrix}\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\\
\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.2)

By the definition of
\widetilde{K}_{t}
in (
3.2
) and the fact that
\Delta\bm{x}_{t}=-B_{t}^{-1}g_{t}
, we have

\begin{pmatrix}I&\bm{0}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\\
\bm{v}_{t,\tau}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I&\bm{0}\end{pmatrix}(I-\widetilde{C}_{t})\begin{pmatrix}I\\
I\end{pmatrix}\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\implies\bm{z}_{t,\tau}=(I-\widetilde{K}_{t})\Delta\bm{x}_{t}=-(I-\widetilde{K}_{t})B_{t}^{-1}g_{t},

which gives us the first part of Lemma
3.5
.

For any
t\geq 0
, by the above conclusion that
\bm{z}_{t,\tau}=(I-\widetilde{K}_{t})B_{t}^{-1}g_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}[(I-\widetilde{K}_{t})B_{t}^{-1}g_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=-\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[(I-\widetilde{K}_{t})B_{t}^{-1}g_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-0.5}]\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}[(I-K_{t})B_{t}^{-1}g_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=-(I-K_{t})B_{t}^{-1}\nabla f_{t}.

(A.3)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}_{t,\tau}-(I-K_{t})\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]

\displaystyle\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:92}}}}{{=}}\;-(I-K_{t})B_{t}^{-1}\nabla f_{t}-(I-K_{t})\mathbb{E}[\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]

\displaystyle\;=\;-(I-K_{t})B_{t}^{-1}\nabla f_{t}+(I-K_{t})\mathbb{E}[B_{t}^{-1}g_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]

\displaystyle\;=\;-(I-K_{t})B_{t}^{-1}\nabla f_{t}+(I-K_{t})B_{t}^{-1}\nabla f_{t}

\displaystyle\;=\;\bm{0}.

We complete the proof of the second part of Lemma
3.5
.

Finally, by the definition of
\widetilde{C}_{t,j}
in (
A.1
), we claim that when
\gamma_{t}=1
, for any
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and any
j\geq 0
, we have

\widetilde{C}_{t,j}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes Y\right)=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes\left((I_{d}-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})Y\right).

(A.4)

In fact, by the property of Kronecker product that
(A\otimes B)(C\times D)=(AC)\otimes(BD)
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{t,j}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes Y\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1-\alpha_{t}&\alpha_{t}\\
(1-\alpha_{t})(1-\beta_{t})&\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes Y-\begin{pmatrix}1-\alpha_{t}&\alpha_{t}\\
1-\alpha_{t}&\alpha_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}Y

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes Y-\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}Y=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes\left((I_{d}-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})Y\right).

By the above property, we then obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}I_{d}\\
I_{d}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{t,\tau-1}\widetilde{C}_{t,\tau-2}\cdots\widetilde{C}_{t,0}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes I_{d}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:101}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}(I_{d}-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})\right).

We then further obtain

\widetilde{K}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def:tilde_K_t}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}I_{d}&\bm{0}\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{C}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}I_{d}\\
I_{d}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{d}&\bm{0}\end{pmatrix}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\otimes\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}(I_{d}-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})\right)\right)=\begin{pmatrix}I&\bm{0}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}(I_{d}-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})\\
\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}(I_{d}-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j})\end{pmatrix}=\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}(I_{d}-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}).

This completes the proof of the final part of Lemma
3.5
.

A.2
Proof of Lemma
3.6

\bullet

Proof of (
3.4
).

Recall the definition of
G_{t}(z)
in (
3.3
). By Cayley–Hamilton Theorem for a
2\times 2
matrix, we have

G_{t}(z)^{2}-\text{Tr}_{t}(z)G_{t}(z)+D_{t}(z)I=\bm{0},

where
\text{Tr}_{t}(z)=\text{trace}(G_{t}(z))
, and
D_{t}(z)=\text{det}(G_{t}(z))
.
Left-multiplying both sides by
\bm{e}_{1}^{\top}G_{t}(z)^{\tau-2}
and right-multiplying by
\bm{1}
yields

p_{t,\tau}(z)=\text{Tr}_{t}(z)p_{t,\tau-1}(z)-D_{t}(z)p_{t,\tau-2}(z),\qquad\forall\ \tau\geq 2.

It is a second-order recursion form, with initialization
p_{t,0}(z)=1
and
p_{t,1}(z)=\bm{e}_{1}^{\top}G_{t}(z)\bm{1}=1-z
.

\bullet

Proof of (
3.5
).

Recall that
\text{Tr}_{t}(z)=\text{trace}(G_{t}(z))=1+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t}-z(1-\alpha_{t}+\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t})
and
D_{t}(z)=\text{det}(G_{t}(z))=(1-\alpha_{t})\beta_{t}(1-z)
. By the definition of
\alpha_{t}
,
\beta_{t}
and
\gamma_{t}
in (
2.4
), we know

\text{Tr}_{t}(z)=\frac{2\nu_{t}-(\nu_{t}+1)z}{\sqrt{\nu_{t}}(\sqrt{\mu_{t}}+\sqrt{\nu_{t}})},\quad\quad D_{t}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{\nu_{t}}-\sqrt{\mu_{t}}}{\sqrt{\nu_{t}}+\sqrt{\mu_{t}}}(1-z).

(A.5)

Also eigenvalues of
G_{t}(z)
are the roots of the following characteristic equation:

\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^{2}-\text{Tr}_{t}(z)\lambda+D_{t}(z)=0.

Hence we obtain (to ease notation, we drop the index
t
for
\lambda
)

\lambda=\frac{\text{Tr}_{t}(z)\pm\sqrt{\text{Tr}_{t}^{2}(z)-4D_{t}(z)}}{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def:Trace_Det}}}{{=}}\frac{2\nu_{t}-(\nu_{t}+1)z\pm\sqrt{(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z^{2}-4\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)z+4\mu_{t}\nu_{t}}}{2\sqrt{\nu_{t}}(\sqrt{\nu_{t}}+\sqrt{\mu_{t}})}.

(A.6)

Recall that
z\in[\mu_{t},1]
and
1\leq\nu_{t}\leq 1/\mu_{t}
(cf. (
2.6
)). Since

16\nu^{2}_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)^{2}-16(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}\mu_{t}\nu_{t}=16\nu_{t}[\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)^{2}-(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}\mu_{t}]=16\nu_{t}(\nu_{t}-\mu_{t})(1-\mu_{t}\nu_{t})\geq 0,

we know
(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z^{2}-4\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)z+4\mu_{t}\nu_{t}
as a function of
z
must have real roots. Below we consider two cases.

•

Case 1:

(\nu_{t}-\mu_{t})(1-\mu_{t}\nu_{t})>0
. In this case,
(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z^{2}-4\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)z+4\mu_{t}\nu_{t}
has two distinctive real roots, which are denoted as
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
.
Plugging the two boundaries
z=\mu_{t}
and
z=1
and observing that the function values are both nonnegative, we know
\mu_{t}\leq z_{1}\leq\frac{2\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)}{(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}}\leq z_{2}\leq 1
. We take the derivative of the larger one in (
A.6
) and obtain

\frac{d\lambda}{dz}=\frac{-(\nu_{t}+1)+\frac{(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z-2\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)}{\sqrt{(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z^{2}-4\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)z+4\mu_{t}\nu_{t}}}}{2\sqrt{\nu_{t}}(\sqrt{\nu_{t}}+\sqrt{\mu_{t}})}.

(A.7)

Note that

\displaystyle((\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z

\displaystyle-2\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1))^{2}-(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}((\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z^{2}-4\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)z+4\mu_{t}\nu_{t})

\displaystyle=(\nu_{t}+1)^{4}z^{2}-4\nu_{t}(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}(\mu_{t}+1)^{2}z+4\nu_{t}^{2}(\mu_{t}+1)^{2}-(\nu_{t}+1)^{4}z^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+4\nu_{t}(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}(\mu_{t}+1)^{2}z-4(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}\mu_{t}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=4\nu_{t}^{2}(\mu_{t}+1)^{2}-4(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}\mu_{t}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=4\nu_{t}(\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)^{2}-\mu_{t}(\nu_{t}+1)^{2})

\displaystyle=4\nu_{t}(\nu_{t}\mu_{t}^{2}+\nu_{t}-\mu_{t}\nu_{t}^{2}-\mu_{t})

\displaystyle=4\nu_{t}(\nu_{t}-\mu_{t})(1-\nu_{t}\mu_{t})\geq 0.

(A.8)

As a result,
(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z-2\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)\geq(\nu_{t}+1)\sqrt{(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z^{2}-4\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)z+4\mu_{t}\nu_{t}}
when
(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z-2\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)\geq 0
, i.e., when
z\geq 2\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)/(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}
.

Case 1a:

z\in[\mu_{t},z_{1}]\cup[z_{2},1]
. In this case,
\lambda
is real. According to (
A.7
) and (
•
‣
A.2
), we have, on
z\in[\mu_{t},z_{1}]
,
d\lambda/dz<0
; on
z\in[z_{2},1]
,
d\lambda/dz>0
. Therefore,
\lambda
decreases on
[\mu_{t},z_{1}]
and increases on
[z_{2},1]
. Hence, the supremum is achieved on
z=\mu_{t}
or
z=1
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},z_{1}]\cup[z_{2},1]}\rho(G_{t}(z))

\displaystyle\leq\text{max}\left\{\frac{\text{Tr}_{t}(1)+\sqrt{\text{Tr}_{t}^{2}(1)-4D_{t}(1)}}{2},\ \frac{\text{Tr}_{t}(\mu_{t})+\sqrt{\text{Tr}_{t}^{2}(\mu_{t})-4D_{t}(\mu_{t})}}{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\text{max}\left\{\frac{\nu_{t}-1}{\sqrt{\nu_{t}}(\sqrt{\mu_{t}}+\sqrt{\nu_{t}})},\ 1-\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{t}}{\nu_{t}}}\right\}=1-\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{t}}{\nu_{t}}}.

Case 1b:

z\in(z_{1},z_{2})
. In this case,
\lambda
is complex, and we have

\sup_{z\in[z_{1},z_{2}]}\rho(G_{t}(z))=\sup_{z\in[z_{1},z_{2}]}\sqrt{D_{t}(z)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def:Trace_Det}}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{\nu_{t}}-\sqrt{\mu_{t}}}{\sqrt{\nu_{t}}+\sqrt{\mu_{t}}}(1-\mu_{t})},

where the last inequality holds since
D_{t}(z)
is a linear function of
z
.

Combining
Case 1a
and
Case 1b
, and observing that

\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{t}}{\nu_{t}}}\right)^{2}-\frac{\sqrt{\nu_{t}}-\sqrt{\mu_{t}}}{\sqrt{\nu_{t}}+\sqrt{\mu_{t}}}(1-\mu_{t})=\frac{(\sqrt{\nu_{t}}-\sqrt{\mu_{t}})\mu_{t}(\nu_{t}-1)}{\nu_{t}(\sqrt{\nu_{t}}+\sqrt{\mu_{t}})}\geq 0,

we obtain
\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}\rho(G_{t}(z))\leq 1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}
for all
t\geq 0
.

•

Case 2:

(\nu_{t}-\mu_{t})(1-\mu_{t}\nu_{t})=0
. In this case,
(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}z^{2}-4\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)z+4\mu_{t}\nu_{t}
has two identical real roots
2\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)/(\nu_{t}+1)^{2}
. Thus, (
A.6
) becomes

\lambda=\frac{\text{Tr}_{t}(z)\pm\sqrt{\text{Tr}_{t}^{2}(z)-4D_{t}(z)}}{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def:Trace_Det}}}{{=}}\frac{2\nu_{t}-(\nu_{t}+1)z\pm\left|(\nu_{t}+1)z-\frac{2\nu_{t}(\mu_{t}+1)}{\nu_{t}+1}\right|}{2\sqrt{\nu_{t}}(\sqrt{\nu_{t}}+\sqrt{\mu_{t}})}.

We can see that the spectral radius of
G_{t}(z)
is piecewise linear, and the supremum is attained on
z=\mu_{t}
or
z=1
. By plugging the two boundaries into the above display, we further conclude the supremum is attained on
z=\mu_{t}
. Thus, we have
\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}\rho(G_{t}(z))\leq 1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}
for all
t\geq 0
.

By Assumption
3.4
, we have
\|Z_{t}^{-1}\|\leq 1/\gamma_{S}
. Therefore, by (
2.6
) and the definition of
\gamma_{t}
in (
2.4
), it follows that

1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{prop_mu_nu}}}{{\leq}}\gamma_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{def:alpha_beta_gamma_t}}}{{=}}1/\sqrt{\mu_{t}\nu_{t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{prop_mu_nu}}}{{\leq}}1/\sqrt{\mu_{t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{prop_mu_nu}}}{{\leq}}1/\sqrt{\gamma_{S}}.

(A.9)

This implies that
1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}\leq 1-\mu_{t}\leq 1-\gamma_{S}\eqqcolon\rho^{\star}
. Combining
Case 1
and
Case 2
completes the proof of (
3.5
).

\bullet

Proof of (
3.6
).

In the following, we will consider three cases.

\bullet\bullet

Case 1:
G_{t}(z)
has two distinct real eigenvalues.
Let us denote the two distinct real eigenvalues of
G_{t}(z)
as
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
, and we know
\text{Tr}_{t}(z)^{2}-4D_{t}(z)>0
. The solution for the recursion (
3.4
) gives us

p_{t,\tau}(z)=A\lambda_{1}^{\tau}+B\lambda_{2}^{\tau}.

We use the initial conditions to determine
A
and
B
:
p_{t,0}(z)=A+B=1
and
p_{t,1}(z)=A\lambda_{1}+B\lambda_{2}=1-z
. By solving these equations, we have

A=\frac{1-z-\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}},\quad\quad B=\frac{\lambda_{1}-(1-z)}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}.

Combining the above two displays, we get the solution of
p_{t,\tau}(z)
as

\displaystyle p_{t,\tau}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1-z-\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\right)\lambda_{1}^{\tau}+\frac{\lambda_{1}-(1-z)}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\lambda_{2}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=(1-z)\frac{\lambda_{1}^{\tau}-\lambda_{2}^{\tau}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\frac{\lambda_{1}^{\tau-1}-\lambda_{2}^{\tau-1}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=(1-z)\sum_{k=0}^{\tau-1}\lambda_{1}^{\tau-1-k}\lambda_{2}^{k}-\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\tau-2}\lambda_{1}^{\tau-2-k}\lambda_{2}^{k},

(A.10)

where the last equality uses the identity that
\frac{a^{n}-b^{n}}{a-b}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a^{n-1-k}b^{k}
for real numbers
a\neq b
. By (
3.5
), we know that
\max\{|\lambda_{1}|,|\lambda_{2}|\}\leq\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}\rho(G_{t}(z))\leq 1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}
for all
t\geq 0
. Then, (
A.2
) leads to the following derivation

\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}|p_{t,\tau}(z)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:27}}}{{\leq}}\tau\left(1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}\right)^{\tau-1}+(\tau-1)\left(1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}\right)^{\tau-2}\leq 2\tau\left(1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}\right)^{\tau-2}.

(A.11)

\bullet\bullet

Case 2:
G_{t}(z)
has two identical real eigenvalues.
In this case, the eigenvalue of
G_{t}(z)
is given by
\lambda=\text{Tr}_{t}(z)/2
and
\text{Tr}_{t}(z)^{2}-4D_{t}(z)=0
. The solution for the recursion (
3.4
) gives us

p_{t,\tau}(z)=(A+B\tau)\lambda^{\tau}.

We use the initial conditions to determine
A
and
B
:
p_{t,0}(z)=A=1
and
p_{t,1}(z)=(A+B)\lambda=1-z
. By solving these equations, we have

A=1,\quad\quad B=\frac{1-z}{\lambda}-1.

Combining the above two displays, we get the solution of
p_{t,\tau}(z)
as

p_{t,\tau}(z)=\left(1+\left(\frac{1-z}{\lambda}-1\right)\tau\right)\lambda^{\tau}.

(A.12)

By (
3.5
),
|\lambda|\leq\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}\rho(G_{t}(z))\leq 1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}
for all
t\geq 0
. Then (
A.12
) leads to the following derivation

\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}|p_{t,\tau}(z)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:30}}}{{\leq}}\tau\lambda^{\tau}+\tau\lambda^{\tau-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:30}}}{{\leq}}2\tau\left(1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}\right)^{\tau-1}.

(A.13)

\bullet\bullet

Case 3:
G_{t}(z)
has two conjugate complex eigenvalues.
Let us denote the two conjugate complex eigenvalues of
G_{t}(z)
by
\lambda_{+}=r(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta)
and
\lambda_{-}=r(\cos\theta-i\sin\theta)
with
r=\sqrt{D_{t}(z)}
and
\cos\theta=\text{Tr}_{t}(z)/(2\sqrt{D_{t}(z)})
, and
\text{Tr}_{t}(z)^{2}-4D_{t}(z)<0
. The solution for the recursion (
3.4
) gives us

p_{t,\tau}(z)=\left(\sqrt{D_{t}(z)}\right)^{\tau}(A\cos(\tau\theta)+B\sin(\tau\theta)).

We use the initial conditions to determine
A
and
B
:
p_{t,0}(z)=A=1
and
p_{t,1}(z)=\sqrt{D_{t}(z)}(A\cos(\theta)+B\sin(\theta))=1-z
. By solving these equations, we have

A=1,\quad\quad B=\frac{2(1-z)-\text{Tr}_{t}(z)}{\sqrt{4D_{t}(z)-\text{Tr}_{t}^{2}(z)}}=\frac{2(1-z)-\text{Tr}_{t}(z)}{2\sqrt{D_{t}(z)}\sin\theta}.

Combining the above two displays, we get the solution of
p_{t,\tau}(z)
as

\displaystyle p_{t,\tau}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\sqrt{D_{t}(z)}\right)^{\tau}\left(\cos(\tau\theta)+\frac{2(1-z)-\text{Tr}_{t}(z)}{\sqrt{4D_{t}(z)-\text{Tr}_{t}(z)^{2}}}\sin(\tau\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=r^{\tau}\cos(\tau\theta)+r^{\tau-1}\left(1-z-\frac{\text{Tr}_{t}(z)}{2}\right)\frac{\sin(\tau\theta)}{\sin\theta}.

(A.14)

Since
\frac{|\sin n\theta|}{|\sin\theta|}\leq n
for all positive integers
n
and any
\theta\in(0,\pi)
,
1
1

1

Consider the summation
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}e^{i(n-1-2k)\theta}=e^{i(n-1)\theta}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}e^{-2ki\theta}=e^{i(n-1)\theta}\cdot\frac{1-e^{-2in\theta}}{1-e^{-2i\theta}}=e^{i(n-1)\theta}\cdot e^{-i(n-1)\theta}\cdot\frac{e^{in\theta}-e^{-in\theta}}{e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\theta}}=\frac{2i\sin n\theta}{2i\sin\theta}=\frac{\sin n\theta}{\sin\theta}
, where the last equality uses Euler’s formula, i.e.
e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\theta}=2i\sin\theta
. Therefore, for all positive integers
n
and
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, we have
\frac{|\sin n\theta|}{|\sin\theta|}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|e^{i(n-1-2k)\theta}|\leq n
.
(
A.2
) leads to the following derivation

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}|p_{t,\tau}(z)|\;

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:33}}}}{{\leq}}\;r^{\tau}+(1-z-0.5\text{Tr}_{t}(z))\,\tau\,r^{\tau-1}=r^{\tau}+(0.5-0.5z+0.5\alpha_{t}\beta_{t}-0.5\beta_{t}-0.5z\alpha_{t}+0.5z\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t})\tau\,r^{\tau-1}

\displaystyle\leq r^{\tau}+(0.5+0.5\alpha_{t}\beta_{t})\tau\,r^{\tau-1}\leq 2\tau r^{\tau-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:spectral_radius_bound}}}{{\leq}}2\tau\,\left(1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}\right)^{\tau-1},

(A.15)

where the second equality is due to
\text{Tr}_{t}(z)=1+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t}-z(1-\alpha_{t}+\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t})
; the third inequality is due to
\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t}\leq 1
by (
2.4
) and (
2.6
); and the fourth inequality is due to
0<\alpha_{t}<1
and
0\leq\beta_{t}<1
.

Combining the results of
Case 1
,
Case 2
,
Case 3
in (
A.11
), (
A.13
), and (
A.2
) completes the proof.

A.3
Proof of Lemma
3.7

Recall that
C_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{C}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
and
\widetilde{C}_{t}=\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\widetilde{C}_{t,j}
, where
\widetilde{C}_{t,j}
is defined in (
A.1
). By the independence of
\{S_{t,j}\}_{j}
, we have
C_{t}=(M_{t})^{\tau}
, where

M_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}1-\alpha_{t}&\alpha_{t}\\
(1-\alpha_{t})(1-\beta_{t})&\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t}\end{pmatrix}\otimes I_{d}-\begin{pmatrix}1-\alpha_{t}&\alpha_{t}\\
(1-\alpha_{t})\gamma_{t}&\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t}\end{pmatrix}\otimes Z_{t}.

(A.16)

We diagonalize each block of
M_{t}
and get the following diagonalized block matrix:

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{t}^{\top}M_{t}\Omega_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}U_{t}^{\top}[M_{t}]_{1,1}U_{t}&U_{t}^{\top}[M_{t}]_{1,2}U_{t}\\
U_{t}^{\top}[M_{t}]_{2,1}U_{t}&U_{t}^{\top}[M_{t}]_{2,2}U_{t}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(1-\alpha_{t})(I-\Lambda_{t})&\alpha_{t}(I-\Lambda_{t})\\
(1-\alpha_{t})(1-\beta_{t})I-(1-\alpha_{t})\gamma_{t}\Lambda_{t}&(\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t})I-\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t}\Lambda_{t}\end{pmatrix},

(A.17)

where
\Omega_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}U_{t}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&U_{t}\end{pmatrix}
,
U_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is orthogonal with
U_{t}U_{t}^{\top}=U_{t}^{\top}U_{t}=I
, and
\Lambda_{t}={\rm diag}(z_{t,1},z_{t,2},\ldots,z_{t,d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is a diagonal matrix with each
(i,i)
-th entry
z_{t,i}
being the
i
-th eigenvalue of
Z_{t}
. Raising
\widehat{M}_{t}
to the power
\tau
yields

\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}=\Omega_{t}^{\top}M_{t}^{\tau}\Omega_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}U_{t}^{\top}[M_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1}U_{t}&U_{t}^{\top}[M_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2}U_{t}\\
U_{t}^{\top}[M_{t}^{\tau}]_{2,1}U_{t}&U_{t}^{\top}[M_{t}^{\tau}]_{2,2}U_{t}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}U_{t}^{\top}[C_{t}]_{1,1}U_{t}&U_{t}^{\top}[C_{t}]_{1,2}U_{t}\\
U_{t}^{\top}[C_{t}]_{2,1}U_{t}&U_{t}^{\top}[C_{t}]_{2,2}U_{t}\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore, by the definition of
K_{t}
, we know that

U_{t}^{\top}K_{t}U_{t}=[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1}+[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2}.

(A.18)

To proceed, we introduce the following lemma.

Lemma A.1
.

Each block of
\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}
is diagonal, that is

[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1}=\begin{pmatrix}[G_{t,1}^{\tau}]_{1,1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&[G_{t,2}^{\tau}]_{1,1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&[G_{t,d}^{\tau}]_{1,1}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2}=\begin{pmatrix}[G_{t,1}^{\tau}]_{1,2}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&[G_{t,2}^{\tau}]_{1,2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&[G_{t,d}^{\tau}]_{1,2}\end{pmatrix},

(A.19)

where we define for each
i\in[d]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,d\}
,
G_{t,i}\coloneqq G_{t}(z_{t,i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
(cf. (
3.3
) for the definition of
G_{t}(\cdot)
).

By (
A.18
) and Lemma
A.1
, for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|K_{t}\|

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in[d]}|[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,1}+[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,2}|=\max_{i\in[d]}|[G_{t}(z_{t,i})^{\tau}]_{1,1}+[G_{t}(z_{t,i})^{\tau}]_{1,2}|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{z\in[\mu_{t},1]}|p_{t,\tau}(z)|\leq 2\tau\left(1-\sqrt{\mu_{t}/\nu_{t}}\right)^{\tau-2},

where the third inequality is from the definition of
p_{t,\tau}(z)
and the last inequality is from Lemma
3.6
. This completes the proof.

A.4
Proof of Lemma
A.1

For simplicity, let us rewrite
\widehat{M}_{t}
in (
A.3
) as

\widehat{M}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}A^{(1)}_{t}&A^{(2)}_{t}\\
A^{(3)}_{t}&A^{(4)}_{t}\end{pmatrix}.

We note that the blocks
A^{(1)}_{t},A^{(2)}_{t},A^{(3)}_{t}
, and
A^{(4)}_{t}
are all diagonal, expressed as
A^{(k)}_{t}=\mathrm{diag}(a^{(k)}_{t,1},\ldots,a^{(k)}_{t,d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
for
k=1,2,3,4
. Then, for each
i\in[d]
, we have

G_{t,i}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{(1)}_{t,i}&a^{(2)}_{t,i}\\
a^{(3)}_{t,i}&a^{(4)}_{t,i}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.20)

We prove the claim by induction on
\tau
.

•

Base case (
\tau=1
).
By (
A.3
), the representation (
A.19
) holds trivially.

•

Induction step.
Assume that (
A.19
) holds for
\tau
. For
\tau+1
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}\widehat{M}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1}&[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2}\\
[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{2,1}&[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{2,2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A^{(1)}_{t}&A^{(2)}_{t}\\
A^{(3)}_{t}&A^{(4)}_{t}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1}A^{(1)}_{t}+[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2}A^{(3)}_{t}&[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1}A^{(2)}_{t}+[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2}A^{(4)}_{t}\\
[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{2,1}A^{(1)}_{t}+[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{2,2}A^{(3)}_{t}&[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{2,1}A^{(2)}_{t}+[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{2,2}A^{(4)}_{t}\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, we have

[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau+1}]_{1,1}=[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1}A^{(1)}_{t}+[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2}A^{(3)}_{t},\qquad[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau+1}]_{1,2}=[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1}A^{(2)}_{t}+[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2}A^{(4)}_{t}.

Since
[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1}
and
[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2}
are diagonal by the induction hypothesis, and
A^{(1)}_{t},A^{(2)}_{t},A^{(3)}_{t},A^{(4)}_{t}
are diagonal, we know both
[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau+1}]_{1,1}
and
[\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau+1}]_{1,2}
are diagonal. Furthermore, for any
i\in[d]
,

([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau+1}]_{1,1})_{i,i}=([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1})_{i,i}a^{(1)}_{t,i}+([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2})_{i,i}a^{(3)}_{t,i},\quad([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau+1}]_{1,2})_{i,i}=([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1})_{i,i}a^{(2)}_{t,i}+([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2})_{i,i}a^{(4)}_{t,i}.

(A.21)

On the other hand, by the definition of
G_{t,i}=G_{t}(z_{t,i})
in (
3.3
), we have

G_{t,i}^{\tau+1}=G_{t,i}^{\tau}G_{t,i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:GA}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,1}&[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,2}\\
[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{2,1}&[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{2,2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a^{(1)}_{t,i}&a^{(2)}_{t,i}\\
a^{(3)}_{t,i}&a^{(4)}_{t,i}\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, for each
i\in[d]
,

[G_{t,i}^{\tau+1}]_{1,1}=[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,1}a^{(1)}_{t,i}+[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,2}a^{(3)}_{t,i},\qquad[G_{t,i}^{\tau+1}]_{1,2}=[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,1}a^{(2)}_{t,i}+[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,2}a^{(4)}_{t,i}.

(A.22)

By the induction hypothesis,
[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,1}=([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,1})_{i,i}
and
[G_{t,i}^{\tau}]_{1,2}=([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau}]_{1,2})_{i,i}
. Substituting these identities into (
A.21
) and (
A.22
) yields

([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau+1}]_{1,1})_{i,i}=[G_{t,i}^{\tau+1}]_{1,1},\qquad([\widehat{M}_{t}^{\tau+1}]_{1,2})_{i,i}=[G_{t,i}^{\tau+1}]_{1,2},\quad\forall\,i\in[d].

This establishes (
A.19
) for
\tau+1
and completes the induction.

A.5
Proof of Theorem
3.8

By Assumption
3.1
, we know
f(\bm{x})
is strongly convex. By the basic property of strong convexity
(
43
)
, we have

\frac{\gamma_{H}}{2}\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{2}\leq f_{t}-f^{\star}\leq\frac{1}{2\gamma_{H}}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}.

(A.23)

Still, by the
\Upsilon_{H}
-Lipschitz continuity of
\nabla f(\bm{x})
implied by Assumption
3.2
, we know from
43
that

\frac{1}{2\Upsilon_{H}}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\leq f_{t}-f^{\star}\leq\frac{\Upsilon_{H}}{2}\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{2}.

(A.24)

Furthermore, by the
\Upsilon_{H}
-Lipschitz continuity of
\nabla f(\bm{x})
, we have the Taylor expansion

f_{t+1}-f^{\star}\leq(f_{t}-f^{\star})+\varphi_{t}\nabla f_{t}^{\top}\bm{z}_{t,\tau}+\frac{\Upsilon_{H}}{2}\varphi_{t}^{2}\|\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\|^{2}.

(A.25)

We then take the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
on both sides of (
A.25
) and obtain

\mathbb{E}[f_{t+1}-f^{\star}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq(f_{t}-f^{\star})+\varphi_{t}\mathbb{E}[\nabla f_{t}^{\top}\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]+\frac{\Upsilon_{H}}{2}\varphi_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}].

(A.26)

Also, by Assumption
3.1
as well as the construction of the Hessian approximate
B_{t}
, we have

\gamma_{H}\leq\lambda_{\min}(B_{t})\leq\lambda_{\max}(B_{t})\leq\Upsilon_{H}.

(A.27)

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
A.26
), we obtain

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}\mathbb{E}[\nabla f_{t}^{\top}\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]

\displaystyle=\varphi_{t}\nabla f_{t}^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=-\varphi_{t}\nabla f_{t}^{\top}(I-K_{t})B_{t}^{-1}\nabla f_{t}

\displaystyle=-\varphi_{t}\nabla f_{t}^{\top}B_{t}^{-1}\nabla f_{t}+\varphi_{t}\nabla f_{t}^{\top}K_{t}B_{t}^{-1}\nabla f_{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{ass:2:c2}}}}{{\leq}}-\frac{\varphi_{t}}{\Upsilon_{H}}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\varphi_{t}}{\gamma_{H}}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\|K_{t}\|\leq-\frac{\varphi_{t}}{2\Upsilon_{H}}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2},

(A.28)

where the second equality follows from Lemma
3.5
and the last inequality follows from Lemma
3.7
as well as the as the condition on
\tau
such that
\|K_{t}\|\leq\gamma_{H}/2\Upsilon_{H}
. To proceed, we establish a uniform bound for
\|\widetilde{K}_{t}\|
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{K}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{def:tilde_K_t}}}}{{=}}\;\left\|\begin{pmatrix}I&\bm{0}\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{C}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}I\\
I\end{pmatrix}\right\|\leq\sqrt{2}\|\widetilde{C}_{t}\|\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\|\widetilde{C}_{t,j}\|

\displaystyle\;\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\left(\|I-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}\|+(1-\alpha_{t})(1-\beta_{t})+(1-\alpha_{t})\gamma_{t}\|\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}\|+(\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\beta_{t})+\alpha_{t}\gamma_{t}\|\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}\|\right)

\displaystyle\;\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\left(\|I-\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}\|+1+\gamma_{t}\|\widetilde{Z}_{t,j}\|\right)

\displaystyle\;\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\left(1+1+\gamma_{t}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{prop_gamma_t}}}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{2}(2+1/\sqrt{\gamma_{S}})^{\tau}\eqqcolon C_{K}.

(A.29)

We now turn to the third term on the right-hand side of (
A.26
), for which we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Upsilon_{H}}{2}

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{z}_{t,\tau}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq\frac{\Upsilon_{H}}{2}\varphi_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|I-\widetilde{K}_{t}\|^{2}\|B_{t}^{-1}\|^{2}\|g_{t}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\quad(\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:inner_transition}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\eqref{ass:2:c2}\\
\eqref{equ:uniform_bound_tK}\end{subarray}}}}{{\leq}}\;\frac{\Upsilon_{H}}{2}\varphi_{t}^{2}\frac{(1+C_{K})^{2}}{\gamma_{H}^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\|g_{t}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq\frac{\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{2}}\varphi_{t}^{2}\left(\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}[\|g_{t}-\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{2}}\varphi_{t}^{2}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{2}}\varphi_{t}^{2}\left(C_{g,1}\|\bm{x}_{t}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|^{2}+C_{g,2}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:46}}}}{{\leq}}\ \frac{\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{2}}\varphi_{t}^{2}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}C_{g,1}}{2\gamma_{H}^{4}}\varphi_{t}^{2}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}C_{g,2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{2}}\varphi_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\gamma_{H}^{2}+C_{g,1})\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{4}}\varphi_{t}^{2}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}C_{g,2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{2}}\varphi_{t}^{2},

(A.30)

where the first inequality follows Lemma
3.5
, and the fourth inequality follows from Assumption
3.2
with
q_{g}=2
. Substituting (
A.5
) and (
A.5
) into (
A.26
) gives us

\mathbb{E}[f_{t+1}-f^{\star}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq f_{t}-f^{\star}-\frac{\varphi_{t}}{2\Upsilon_{H}}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\left(\gamma_{H}^{2}+C_{g,1}\right)\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{4}}\varphi_{t}^{2}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}C_{g,2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{2}}\varphi_{t}^{2}.

(A.31)

Since
\varphi_{t}=C_{\varphi}/(t+1)^{\varphi}
and
\varphi\in\left(1/2,1\right]
, there exists
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
,
\frac{\left(\gamma_{H}^{2}+C_{g,1}\right)\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{4}}\varphi_{t}\leq\frac{1}{4\Upsilon_{H}}
. Then, for all
t\geq t_{0}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[f_{t+1}-f^{\star}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq f_{t}-f^{\star}-\frac{\varphi_{t}}{4\Upsilon_{H}}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\Upsilon_{H}(1+C_{K})^{2}C_{g,2}}{2\gamma_{H}^{2}}\varphi_{t}^{2}.

(A.32)

Since
\sum_{t}\varphi_{t}=\infty
and
\sum_{t}\varphi_{t}^{2}<\infty
, we apply the Robbins-Siegmund Theorem
(
17
, Theorem 1.3.12)
, and conclude that
f_{t}-f^{\star}
converges to a finite random variable, and
\sum_{t=t_{0}}^{\infty}\varphi_{t}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}<\infty
almost surely. This implies that
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\|\nabla f_{t}\|=0
almost surely; and thus
\liminf_{t\to\infty}(f_{t}-f^{\star})=0
almost surely by (
A.23
). Since we have already known that
f_{t}-f^{\star}
converges almost surely, the conclusion can be strengthened to
\lim_{t\to\infty}f_{t}-f^{\star}=0
. Again, we apply (
A.23
) and obtain
\lim_{t\to\infty}\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{x}^{\star}
almost surely. We complete the proof of Theorem
3.8
.

Appendix B
Proofs of Section
4

Throughout the section, we exchangeably denote
a_{t}=O(b_{t})
and
a_{t}\lesssim b_{t}
to ease notation. We also use
\bm{1}_{\{\cdot\}}
to denote the indicator function.

B.1
Proof of Lemma
4.1

\bullet

Convergence of
B_{t}
.

We first establish the almost-sure convergence of
B_{t}
. By the definition of
B_{t}
in (
2.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|B_{t}-B^{\star}\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i})+\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(\nabla^{2}f_{i}-\nabla^{2}f^{\star})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i})\right\|+\left\|\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(\nabla^{2}f_{i}-\nabla^{2}f^{\star})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i})\right\|+\frac{\Upsilon_{L}}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\|\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{x}^{\star}\|,

(B.1)

where the last inequality follows from Assumption
3.1
. The second term on the right-hand side of (
B.1
) converges to
0
almost surely, which is the direct consequence of Theorem
3.8
together with the Stolz–Cesàro theorem. For the first term on the right-hand side of (
B.1
), we note from Assumption
3.1
that
\mathbb{E}[\|H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i}\|^{2}_{F}]\lesssim 1
. Since
\mathbb{E}[H_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=\nabla^{2}f_{t}
, we know
(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
is a martingale difference sequence. Thus,
(\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i})/(i+1),\,\mathcal{F}_{t-1})_{t\geq 1}
is a martingale satisfying

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\frac{1}{i+1}(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\frac{1}{i+1}(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i})\right\|^{2}_{F}\right]=\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{(i+1)^{2}}\|H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i}\|^{2}_{F}\right]<\infty,

where the equality uses the orthogonality of martingale differences under the Frobenius inner product, so that all cross terms have zero expectation. The above display suggests that
(\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i})/(i+1))_{t\geq 1}
is
L^{2}
-bounded, and hence
\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\frac{1}{i+1}(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i})
converges almost surely by Doob’s martingale convergence theorem
(
25
, Corollary 2.2)
. Applying Kronecker’s Lemma gives us
\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(H_{i}-\nabla^{2}f_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\rightarrow}}0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. This shows that both two terms in (
B.1
) converge to
0
almost surely; thus,
B_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\rightarrow}}B^{\star}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

\bullet

Convergence of
(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t},\gamma_{t})
.
